Written Exemplars

Sample 1 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

ZZEXP Rainbow’s End I ZZEXPEND

Topic: How does Rainbow’s End suggest that everything will be alright?
In the 2005 Indigenous play ‘Rainbows End’, playwright Jane Harrison suggests everything will be alright for the Dear family, despite the challenges they combat. This is shown through the portrayal of self-determination, coupled with the continued hope and optimism of the Dears. However, the heavily damaging colonial attitudes to the Indigenous community act as a constant threat looming over the community and their futures.
Throughout the play, Harrison highlights the unwavering self-determination of the Indigenous community, suggesting everything will be alright due to the strong resolve of the Dear family. This is depicted most vividly through the growth of Gladys, initially reserved and acquiescent, but steadily becoming more outspoken and steadfast in her beliefs for a better future. This culminates in Gladys taking a bold stand for the indigenous community at the town meeting, stating “I’m not an interloper – I belong here! - this is my land!”. Gladys’ open defiance in the meeting, challenging cultural norms and expectations, acts as a pivotal switch from Gladys’ belief everything will be alright, to taking direct action in pursuit of a better future for her family. Similarly, Dolly’s stoicism and resilience in the face of adversity and her assault serve as an emphatic representation of selfdetermination. After her assault, Dolly’s honest and true nature in showing her baby to Errol, despite severe shame, depicts her growing as an individual through hardship and pain through these qualities of self-determination. Stating “Yes, I’m okay” when questioned by Errol about her baby, Dolly embraces her struggles, remaining determined and continuing to seek a better future for herself and her baby. Overall, Harrison suggests everything will be alright for the Dear family, despite great hardship, through the continued determination of both Gladys and Dolly.
Harrison further suggests that everything will be alright due to the stalwart hope of the Dear family, embodied through Papa Dear, dream sequences and the prospect of education. Throughout, Papa Dear acts as a symbol of a positive future and hope for the family and broader Indigenous community, travelling across Australia fighting for their rights. This is shown through Nan, who states “its Papa Dears mission to make things better for the Aboriginal people”, suggesting that Papa Dear acts as a figurehead and an unseen force for good in the Indigenous community. Similarly, Papa Dear exists as a projection of hope through dream sequences with Gladys, who observes “Papa Dear, in old fashioned hat and coat, dances in, throws his hat on the hatstand, kisses Gladys on the top of the head, and dances out”. This suggests an omnipresent quality of Papa Dear as a symbol of hope for the members of the Dear family; as a constant reminder that everything will be alright. Further, the prospect of education offers hope for the future for Dolly, and is first presented in the form of encyclopaedia’s from Errol, to which Gladys exclaims “my daughter, a graduate”. This is further propagated by Dolly’s ambitions of becoming a nurse in Melbourne, as a catapult to a better and more prosperous future. Ultimately, Harrison suggests that the unwavering hope of the Dear family, through Papa Dear and the prospect of education, are a reminder that everything will be alright.
However, Harrison also indicates the highly destructive nature of colonial attitudes, and their ability to prevent the Dear family from a better future, as a direct challenge to the sentiment that everything will be alright. Harrison portrays this primarily through the housing inspector, representing a hostile Australian government policy and its corrosive effect on the lives and futures of the Indigenous communities. This is demonstrated through the removal of Aunty Esthers children at the behest of the inspector, and further the repeated visits he makes to the Dear family home. Stating “your whites are so white, with river water no less”, the inspector highlights to close scrutiny of every detail of the Dear’s lives, and the constant threat to their future looming over them. Similarly, Harrison suggests the continued resentment of the broader community actively works to dissuade the Dears and Indigenous community from seeking a better future. This is displayed through the town council, whilst attempting to silence Gladys challenging societal norms, stating “the Chair does not recognise this…. Eject this interloper!”. Further, Harrison displays the negative affects of such attitudes through Dolly, exclaiming “it’ll never change…. Some things never do” after being scrutinised by Nancy at the Shepparton ball. Overall, Harrison suggests that colonial attitudes can directly challenge and threaten the hopes and self-determination of the Dear family and broader indigenous community, and further the prospects of everything being alright.
On balance, Harrison displays the potential for everything to be alright for the Dear family and broader Indigenous community through the qualities of self-determination and hope, but also the constant threat of colonial attitudes to the future.

Sample 2 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

ZZEXP Rainbow’s End II ZZEXPEND

Topic: ’Harrison’s play celebrates characters who show strength through adversity.’ Discuss.
Set in Victorian town, Shepparton, Jane Harrison’s 2007 play, Rainbow’s End, explores the lives of three generations of Koori women as they navigate the setbacks and challenges faced by First Nations people in 1950s Victoria. At a time where the government regulates the policies of Assimilation of the Indigenous community, these women are vulnerable to the impacts of racism, colonisation and suppression of knowledge. But despite facing discrimination at every turn, Harrison commemorates their resilience in the face of adversity. The women are able to become symbols of hope as they refuse to allow their struggles to define them.
Throughout Rainbow’s End, the struggle for the Indigenous community to pursue their dreams due to racial prejudice is highlighted in order to spotlight their ability to overcome it. This is demonstrated in scene ten in the bank managers office. Gladys tries to apply Dolly for a job as the bank teller, assuring the bank manager that she is fit for the job by advocating for her brains and wits. But despite Dolly being a good candidate, the manager is hesitant about letting her apply. He argues that, “reliability is important,” implying that Dolly is unreliable despite never having met her. He makes this assumption due to his personal prejudice to people of Dolly’s heritage. This demonstrates the racial discrimination that limits opportunities of First Nation’s people. Regardless of the bank manager’s doubts, Gladys pushes for the job as she is reminded of Dolly’s words, “have you learnt not to be shamed by them?”. Through this, Harrison highlights how the women’s power to overcome setbacks comes from within themselves. She champions these women for their ability to stand up for themselves in spite of the racially biased attitudes that are meant to limit them. This theme of hope is prevalent throughout the play and intends to inspire strength from within to tackle problems.
Moreover, Harrison explores the intricacies of cultural identities and belonging, highlighting the strong connection to culture despite the colonisation that the characters face. Nan Dear is a compelling example of this. Nan’s frequent allusions to spirits or warnings from her “ancestors” demonstrate the strong indigenous traditions that survive and carry on even after the effects of colonisation. Intergenerational wisdom is significant in indigenous culture and Nan Dear’s frequent references to guidance from her ancestors attempts to pass that down through her children, who may continue to succumb to assimilation but nevertheless is still deeply rooted in their cultural identities. The reminders of tradition allows Harrison to assert that cultural identity cannot be erased and is represented through characters like Nan Dear who have been through cultural erasure but continues to be a strong image of indigenous culture.
Furthermore, resilience is present in overcoming adversity from the suppression of knowledge that characters throughout the play go through. Gladys is a representation of this as it is revealed to the reader that Gladys is illiterate. This was due to the assimilation policy that separated Gladys from her family and forced her into working at a young age rather than getting an education. Harrison demonstrates Gladys overcoming this in the last scene of the play where Gladys delivers a speech at the public meeting. Not only does she deliver it flawlessly, she delivers it with confidence, which Gladys has mentioned that she “gets nervous in front of people.” Harrison acknowledges the optimistic and determined attitude that Gladys uses as a tool to fight for her ambitions despite the systemic racism that has denied her basic human rights. Through Gladys Harrison aims to write about the ‘unsung’ heroes who were not expected to be a hero but was able to become one in spite of the adversity that she faces.
In conclusion, “Rainbows End” champions the characters for their ability to overcome adversity due to their unwavering determination, offering a powerful testament to the power of human spirit when up against hardship. Through the use of quotes and direct references to the characters, Harrison tells a compelling story that aims to bring hope to the audience, reminding them that the difficulties that humanity faces is incomparable to the resilience that lies within each of us.

Sample 3 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

ZZEXP Rainbow’s End III ZZEXPEND

Topic: ‘Least here we do things our way- no one breathin’ down our necks’
Home is central to identity in Rainbow’s End. To what extent do you agree?
Amongst the racism and oppression, it is clear that ‘home’ is of the utmost importance to the identity of Harrison’s Indigenous characters. Set against a backdrop of white Australian superiority in the 1950s, ‘Rainbow’s End’ by Jane Harrison explores the central theme of ‘home’ in the context of identity. For the three generational main characters, their ‘humpy’ alongside the riverbank of the Mooroopna flats is integral to the make up of who they are and essential for Harrison to showcase the strength and resilience needed for First Nations to fight their marginalised status. Beyond the physical ‘home’, identity is shaped by the family and culture surrounding the characters through the lens of white and Indigenous Australians.
Throughout the first part of the play, ‘home’ is physically represented as the ‘humpy’, a hut with a dirt floor and hessian-covered walls. Despite the poverty of the main character’s living conditions, it isn’t the place where they sleep that is important to them as much as the culture surrounding them. For the character of Nan, "the Murray…Is your place isn’t it” due to the immense sense of belonging she feels after her oppressive experience with the Cummeragunja mission and the close proximity of her Aboriginal ‘family.’ In this way Harrison highlights the role culture and a physical ‘home’ plays in an individual’s identity. In addition, the pride taken in the appearance and homeliness of the ‘humpy’ is a further aspect of identity illustrated by Harrison. When in the play it comes time for the inspector to visit to the ‘humpy’, Harrison has Nan and Gladys ‘nervously’ straightening the cushions and draws attention to the ‘beautiful work’ of the crocheted pillows, emphasising the importance of ‘home’ to the characters. This is brought to the attention of the audience again during the move to the ‘white and featureless’ Rumberala housing, and the characters immediately think of ‘making curtains’ as a way to reassociate their identity with the new housing. The contrast between the ‘home’ of the white Australian characters such as Errol and Nancy, and the housing in Rumbalara and the flats is reflective of the identities of the characters and how they feel entitled to act. For example, Nancy is portrayed as wealthy and privileged and therefore acts in a privileged and entitled way, whilst the main characters are substantially underprivileged and act humble, with kindness despite adversities.
The playwright suggests ‘home’ is more than a physical establishment but extends into a ‘home’ found within family and culture. The notion of family and culture as a ‘home’ is a central theme throughout the play, explicitly explored between the misalignment of Errol and Dolly’s definition of a ‘home’. For the character of Errol, he believes a ‘home’ is a place to live, where only the wealth and geographical opportunities are relevant, however, Harrison utilises his conversation with Dolly to pinpoint to ideal that “a real home is where people are looking out for each other,” regardless of how many ‘presents’ are under the tree. Furthermore, Errol reflects on the differences of their families, expressing to Dolly that his Dad “expects me to call him sir” and ultimately concluding that despite the poverty of the flat’s he bets ‘you have more fun than we do’. This conversation was pivotal to underlining the impact of a ‘home’ with a supportive family and rich culture in creating a luster for life and powerful sense of identity.
For the characters in the play, ‘home’ is closely intertwined with colonial suppression, the white Australian’s control over Aboriginal housing had a heavy influence on the identity of the characters. Throughout the play, Harrison makes reference to a difference in the perception of Aboriginals from the flat’s and ‘townie types of cross-over Aborigines’ depicting those who lived in town as slightly better off, with better opportunities. This differentiation underpins the impact of location on identity as the ‘white fellas’ feel the Indigenous characters need to ‘be absorbed into the community’ in order to be ‘assimilated’ and forced to act in alignment with white culture. The control that the white characters, such as the inspector, assert over housing is detrimental to the identity of the characters, it withdraws their control and forces them to abandon their culture in order to be accepted. Moreover, Harrison suggests that a ‘home’ can affect social mobility, Gladys in particular is obsessed with ‘fitting in’ and being accepted and it becomes a large part of her identity. The ‘white gloves’ and ‘Auntys shoes’ are a symbol of her need to assimilate to white culture, acting in direct contrast to the Aboriginal culture that makes up her ‘home’ and thereby her identity.
Throughout the entirety of the play, Harrison brings forth the different types of ‘home’, both physical and as a culmination of family and culture. In this way it is evident to the audience that home is closely entwined with the identity of all the characters in the play, from Errol who has never experienced a ‘home’ within a family, to Dolly whom does not associate a ‘real home’ with a fancy establishment but instead with a loving family and cultural traditions. The exploration into the impacts of oppressive attempts from the ‘white fellas’ to force ‘assimilation’, displays how a ‘home’ is more than a roof over one’s head, but instead an embodiment of an individual’s unique identity as formed by relationships, culture, pride and resilience.

Sample 4 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

ZZEXP Rainbow’s End IV ZZEXPEND

Topic: Harrison’s play celebrates characters who show strength through adversity. Discuss.
Jane Harrison’s play Rainbows End is a powerful exploration of the lives of three generations of Indigenous women, Nan Dear, her daughter Gladys, and granddaughter Dolly, in 1950s rural Australia. Harrison presents their daily struggles with poverty, limited access to education, and systemic racism, not to victimise them, but to celebrate their resilience and determination. Through her portrayal of these women, Harrison critiques the entrenched hierarchies that marginalise Indigenous communities, while affirming the strength required to survive and resist. Ultimately, the play celebrates the capacity of Indigenous women to endure adversity, even as it recognises the costs of their survival.
Harrison portrays how racial discrimination restricts Indigenous Australians’ opportunities and dreams. This is evident in Scene Ten, when Gladys visits the bank in the hope of securing Dolly a position as a teller. Gladys proudly emphasises her daughter’s academic success, noting that Dolly has completed her “leaving certificate” and is “top of her class,” in an attempt to counteract the stereotypes imposed upon Indigenous people. However, the bank manager dismisses Dolly’s achievements, questioning how she would “fit in” and whether she could be “relied on” to attend work daily, despite never having met her. Through the portrayal of the bank manager, Harrison highlights how deep-seated structural racism embedded in Australian institutions causes harmful stereotypes that prevent Indigenous communities from fulfilling their full potential in society, denying them equal access to employment. By juxtaposing the women’s ambitions with the bank manager’s prejudices, Harrison critiques the injustice of a culture that confines Indigenous families to “picking” and other forms of low-status labour regardless of merit. Yet she also affirms Gladys’ resilience, showing her refusal to abandon her daughter’s dreams despite repeated setbacks. In doing so, Harrison not only exposes how systemic racism denies equality but also celebrates the persistence of Indigenous women who continue to assert dignity and demand recognition against such barriers.
Harrison exposes the entrenched poverty endured by Indigenous families to critique the systemic marginalisation of First Nations communities. Rather than presenting poverty as a personal failing, Harrison illustrates how social and economic exclusion force the Dear family into degrading conditions and low-cost labour, while simultaneously highlighting their resilience in preserving dignity and hope. Nan Dear’s ironic description of their riverbank “humpy” as “our own little palace” re-frames deprivation as pride, transforming humiliation into humour. Similarly, Gladys expresses frustration at Dolly’s reliance on “hand-me-downs,” revealing her determination to secure independence for her daughter. By infusing moments of humour and defiance into depictions of poverty, Harrison refuses to allow Indigenous identity to be defined by shame. Instead, she asserts the capacity of women like Nan and Gladys to maintain agency and self-respect in the face of adversity. Dolly’s dream sequence after her visit to the town tip symbolises a longing for a future beyond dispossession, foregrounding aspirations that colonial systems sought to deny. Through these portrayals, Harrison critiques structural inequalities, particularly the exploitation of Indigenous people as fruit pickers and scavengers, while simultaneously celebrating the resilience and strength of Indigenous women to endure and reimagine possibility. Thus, Harrison’s authorial intent lies not only in exposing the material realities of Indigenous poverty but also in affirming the power of humour, pride and aspiration as acts of survival against systemic injustice.
Knowledge is presented in the play as both a pathway to empowerment and a means of representing the differences in wisdom. Gladys has a strong belief that schooling will elevate Dolly beyond ingrained disadvantage, insisting that she “get an education” so she can “be someone.” This determination highlights her strength as a mother, willing to challenge generational cycles of poverty. Harrison deliberately frames this ambition with irony: while Dolly’s academic success is undeniable, she remains judged through stereotypes and institutional racism, suggesting that education alone cannot dismantle systemic barriers. At the same time, Harrison complicates the notion of knowledge by staging contrasts between Indigenous oral tradition and Western authority. When Errol dismisses Dolly’s experience with snakes, Gladys’ clarification that it was a harmless “carpet snake” undermines the authority of the encyclopedia and asserts that lived experience offers forms of wisdom inaccessible to formal schooling. By juxtaposing these knowledge systems, Harrison critiques the way white institutions privileged their own frameworks as universal while marginalising Indigenous perspectives. Her authorial intent is doubled to expose how education in the 1950s was arranged by race and class, yet also to celebrate the resilience of families like the Dears, who embraced education without relinquishing pride in their cultural knowledge. Harrison both honours the courage of Indigenous families who value education as a tool for advancement and critiques the system of schooling planted between classes in the 1950s.
Harrison’s Rainbow’s End critiques the systemic racism that restricted Indigenous Australians while celebrating the resilience of women who refused to be silenced. By exposing institutional prejudice, entrenched poverty and competing forms of knowledge, she highlights both the barriers that denied equality and the strength of Indigenous families who asserted dignity despite them. In doing so, Harrison affirms that hope, humour and cultural pride are not just survival strategies but powerful acts of resistance against injustice.

Sample 5 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

ZZEXP Rainbow’s End V ZZEXPEND

Topic: ’Rainbow’s End is a bitter condemnation of colonisation.’ Discuss.
Cemented within a world enveloped by overpowering white culture, Rainbows End seeks to explore the lengths of condemnation expressed by Indigenous Australians, juxtaposed with the desire to be included. Within the play, Harrison blatantly reveals consistent resistance and dislike aimed towards white authority yet contrasts this with younger generations who desire integration and inclusion in society. By depicting Indigenous women in this way, Harrison can characterise older generations as resistant to white knowledge and eternally furious at the erasure of Indigenous culture yet depicts younger characters as open to colonisation to succeed in their corrupted society. Overall, Harrison criticises the unjust dilution of Indigenous culture, simultaneously advocating for Indigenous Australians to continue fighting to be heard over white dominance.
Harrison calculatingly embeds Indigenous resistance throughout the play, through unwavering denial, which represents a resilience to maintain Indigenous culture, despite attempts to silence it. Harrison evidence this though Nan Dear showing her despise for Errol’s encyclopedias, asking him what it says, “in that there encyclops about Aboriginals”. Harrison’s lexical choice of characterising Nan through her informal and grammatically incorrect language of “that there” and “encyclops”, illustrates Nan’s refusal to submit to white assistance in education, as the encyclopedias are based off Eurocentric knowledge, maintaining her stance in continuing Aboriginal knowledge and culture no matter the state of her academics. Nan Dear also directs this hate towards Errol, as he is the delivery boy for them, and is white, as she sees him as a messenger for white authority, being cautious of letting him get too close. Harrison lambasts the erasure of Indigenous knowledge, yet cautiously condemns the anger being taken out on unrelated and innocent white individuals. Harrison juxtaposes this condemnation of colonisation through dream sequences, when Gladys begins to dream of the possibility of Dolly becoming “a graduate”. Gladys’ willingness to accept and integrate into the complexities of white society in order to provide her daughter with a proper education, contrasts that of Nan’s ideologies relating to white assistance, demonstrating the differences in generational beliefs. As encyclopedias are based on white knowledge, this further illustrates Gladys’ will to accept their knowledge as superior to succeed. The dream sequence represents Gladys’ subconscious suggestion that her daughter may never be able to be “a graduate” due to her needing to follow Nan’s beliefs as the eldest of the family, being constrained to the limitations rightful morals to uphold Indigenous independence. Harrison champions the pursuit of education despite society restraining full ability yet criticises the neglect of protecting Indigenous culture and forgetting true identity.
Harrison impatiently lambasts the evident erasure of Indigenous culture, through white authorities directly and indirectly pushing it, which exposes their never-ending desire to abolish Aboriginal knowledge and existence. Harrison represents this through the humpy inspector asking if Gladys has heard of “the concept of assimilation”, as a promise that “things will change.” The contradictory nature of the inspectors’ intentions and promises represent white ignorance towards Indigenous perspectives and opinions, as the inspector didn’t let Gladys answer if she has heard of the “concept” of assimilation, continuing to talk about it as if she isn’t there. The inherent dominance from the inspector pushing for the absorption of Indigenous Australians into white society conveys the collective idea of abolishment, and then stating that “knowledge is power” continues to falsely represent the freedom of Indigenous Australians white authorities distribute. Harrison demonises the dominance of white imposed hierarchies that put Indigenous Australians at an unfair disadvantage, keeping them there without proper consultation. Harrison further bolsters this through the stage direction of Gladys and Nan “exchange[ing] worried looks”, after the inspector’s suggestion. Their “worried looks” emphasise the desire to remain where they are, away from the “mercy of the manager” which Nan Dear recalls from her traumatic days of living at a mission. The family can easily accept help from white authorities by submitting to their dominance yet refuse to so they can maintain their independence and keep their cultural heritage and identity intact, living of their own free will. Their desire to stay at their humpy and not receive better living conditions demonstrates their appreciation for the opportunity to live by themselves and the gratitude for where they are, no matter the difficulties that surround these unfavourable living circumstances. Harrison bolsters the idea of maintaining Indigenous heritage but questions the resistance towards white assistance if it is at the expense of health and safety.
Harrison diametrically contradicts the stereotypical resentment towards colonisation through Indigenous youth desiring to submit to white dominance, which raises questions about the rightful path to follow when aiming to achieve Indigenous independence. Harrison depicts this through Dolly’s desire to be included in white society, when Dolly is attending a dance in Shepparton, and exclaims how “everyone’s going to be there”. Dolly being the youngest of the family is the most exposed to whitewashing and diluted Indigenous culture, hence her powerful ambition to attend dances and wear Eurocentric 1950s outfits and accessories, to be like “everyone” who’s going “to be there”. Her outfit is symbolic of the tensions between her socio-economic status and her dreams, acting as a constant reminder of her inability to completely be accepted in white society. Harrison praises the confidence of Indigenous youth to break free from limiting social and gender norms in order to achieve one’s dreams yet warns against the dangers of being denied acceptance from such rebellion. Harrison further reinforces a rebellion in the opposition to white superiority through Gladys’ dialogue when she dares to believe that Dolly can succeed by not letting “her address stop her in life”. Gladys’ rhetorical question towards Nan exemplifies the difference and experience of generations, Gladys believing Indigenous women can succeed without the acceptance of white people. She is met by Nan Dear’s cold response telling her to “get a grip”, snapping her back to reality and reminding her of the fact that they are a minority that is disadvantaged in a backwards society that will never favour them. Gladys daring to stray from the wisdom of her mother represents an underlying feeling of hope for change, despite claims and actions that proves change is unlikely to occur. Gladys and Dolly don’t harbour severe resentment towards their society like Nan Dear, as they have less experience with unwavering discrimination. It outlines their desires to blend into the white community at the expense of their autonomy, in order to gain increased access and succeed in achieving a better life than what they have. Harrison champions the unwavering objective of striving to be better in one’s life, while cautioning of the implications of aiming too high and ignoring the safety provided by one’s community.
Conclusively, Harrison continuously exhibits justified resistance to colonisation of Indigenous culture yet remains hopeful that younger generations may have the courage to break the discriminatory cycle. Harrison encapsulates this through continued vilification of white knowledge and education, and the injustice of institutional authority, while postulating a juxtaposed change in views through the acceptance of colonisation within younger generations. Harrison ultimately condemns the unjust erasure of Indigenous culture and identity, while pushing the notion that the amalgamation of cultures may lead to increased opportunities for Indigenous Australians and should not be seen as an entirely demonised occurrence.

Sample 6 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay with Annotations

ZZEXP Rainbow’s End VI with Annotations ZZEXPEND

Topic: How does Harrison explore the concept of ’home’ in Rainbow’s End?
Introduction

In Rainbow’s End, Jane Harrison explores the concept of home by presenting Australia through the lens of three generations of Indigenous Australian women (1). Set in Shepparton/Mooroopna during the 1950s, against the backdrop of assimilation policies and the aftermath of the Cummeragunja walk off (2), the play sets up home as a source of conflict between cultures. For white Australia home is a house full of modern conveniences, and therefore superior to a humpy on the floodplains, that needs to be monitored by government inspectors (3). Through three generations of Indigenous women, Harrison proposes that a house containing a nuclear family is not the same thing as a true home built on harmony with nature, connection to Country and community, and that full reconciliation depends on white Australia learning that difference (4).
Annotations:

(1) Naming the structural device (three generations of women) in the first sentence signals to a marker that the essay has a clear analytical architecture before a single quote appears — the body paragraphs can then be trusted to deliver on this promise.

(2) Historical specificity like this is worth far more than a vague "in the past" reference. The Cummeragunja walk-off is real context that grounds Nan Dear’s later dialogue; using it here shows research beyond the play itself, which examiners reward as sophistication.

(3) This sentence defines the opposing side of the argument (the white Australian view of home) before the essay has even reached a body paragraph — setting up the contention as a genuine debate rather than a one-sided assertion.

(4) A strong contention doesn’t just answer the question, it makes an arguable claim with a "because" attached — here, that reconciliation is conditional on white Australia changing, not just Indigenous families adapting. That specificity is what separates a contention from a topic restatement.
Body Paragraph 1

White Australia’s idea that a house makes a home is set against the Indigenous idea that home is mostly about staying connected to country and culture (1). Older Indigenous generations belong to a time before the government controlled Indigenous people’s independence through assimilation policies aiming to eradicate their culture, policies that included the Stolen Generations, that were only wound back in the late 1960s (2). For Nan Dear, home is the Murray River and Cummeragunja, the mission where managers "forced us to leave... to leave Cummeragunja. Our home," she says. Through this, Harrison shows that Nan Dear’s memories of Cummeragunja aren’t just nostalgic but political, tied to the loss of autonomy under assimilation policies (3). Her fierce independence, evidenced by her "we sink or swim on our own," is why she’d rather live in a humpy, insisting "it’s ours." The humpy, respectfully described as "clean and homely" by Harrison in her setting descriptors, is a symbol of self-determination, its ownership set against the government-controlled housing offered to Indigenous people elsewhere (4). But for Nan Dear, home means independence and agency, a connection to land and heritage, rather than four walls or new fittings. Even the floods don’t shake this as she accepts that "that’s nature," in contrast to Gladys and Dolly, who keep "fighting against how things are." Harrison respects a generation willing to endure struggle, if it means holding on to agency and connection to country. As such, home is more than a place for one family to live, but a broader cultural, historical and geographical concept, and Nan Dear’s contentment in the humpy shows this isn’t sacrifice, but freedom (5).
Annotations:

(1) The topic sentence names both sides of the tension (white materialism versus Indigenous connection) rather than just describing Nan Dear — this keeps the paragraph anchored to the actual question about "home" rather than drifting into a character study.

(2) Embedding a factual clause about assimilation policy and the Stolen Generations mid-sentence, rather than as a separate statement, is an efficient way to fold context into analysis without stalling the argument.

(3) This is the paragraph’s strongest analytical move: reframing a quote that could be read as simple grief ("our home") as evidence of a political argument about autonomy. Markers reward this kind of reinterpretation over surface-level quote description.

(4) Citing Harrison’s own stage direction ("clean and homely") as evidence is a technique many students overlook — playwrights’ descriptive language is authorial comment in a way dialogue alone isn’t, and using it shows attention to the whole text, not just spoken lines.

(5) The paragraph’s final sentence links back to the contention’s key terms ("more than a place... but a broader... concept") rather than simply summarising the plot — this is what makes a concluding sentence do analytical work instead of just closing a paragraph.
Body Paragraph 2

Harrison also asserts that change under government control has only ever meant less independence for Indigenous Australians (1). This distrust of government comes through when the Inspector announces that "things will change," regarding the humpy’s deficient living conditions such as sanitation and no electric lights, a statement met with "worried looks between Nan Dear and Gladys," as Harrison’s stage directions depict. This is clearest in the ironically titled "Home Sweet Home" scene (2), where the radio spruiks the move to Rumbalara as "the most vigorous attempt yet to solve Aboriginal housing," but Harrison’s stage directions describe the houses as "concrete, small, white and featureless," and it’s here that Nan Dear develops a cough (3). This is not the kind of house they expected, Harrison even portraying the Dears having to sleep on the roof during the hotter nights. When Gladys turns her want for decent housing into activism, over the radio she declares that she’s going "to fix my own house," because "us Aboriginals are not welcome in the townships" and are left with nothing but "concrete humpies" the council dares to "call… houses." Harrison’s point is that Indigenous people must fight just to get a decent home, and that fight shouldn’t cost them their identity or their right to decide what happens within their own walls (4). Thus, Harrison demonstrates that Indigenous people cannot rely on the white man’s government to improve living conditions or recognise their land rights, as Gladys declares unequivocally, "I belong here - this is my land!"
Annotations:

(1) Opening with an authorial claim ("Harrison also asserts...") rather than a plot description keeps the analysis operating at the level of what the playwright is arguing, which is what Section A criteria are actually assessing.

(2) Naming the scene title and noting its irony in the same breath is a compact, efficient piece of structural analysis — it shows the essay is tracking the play’s own use of dramatic irony, not just quoting from memory.

(3) Pairing the government’s own promotional language ("the most vigorous attempt") against Harrison’s flat, clinical stage direction ("concrete, small, white and featureless") is a strong juxtaposition technique — letting the text’s own contradictions do the analytical work rather than over-explaining them.

(4) This sentence does double duty: it states the play’s argument and pre-empts a counterpoint (that better housing might have been enough), which shows awareness of the topic’s complexity without derailing into a full alternate paragraph.
Body Paragraph 3

Harrison also argues that modern generations of Indigenous women can and should aspire to go to universities, even if that means leaving those you love in the rural areas (1). Dolly’s role is to reflect the agency of choice for Indigenous women, not because a white man wants to marry her, but because of the educational opportunities there. When Errol offers Dolly "a better life" in Melbourne, she refuses because that would mean leaving her Nan, her mother, and her community in Shepparton and Mooroopna. With good intentions, he wants Dolly to have "a real home," not understanding that this "world" isn’t hers, because for Dolly, "it don’t matter if the floors are dirt." Their life, she insists, echoing Nan Dear, "isn’t perfect but... it’s ours" (2). Harrison shows the reconciliation between white and Indigenous Australia through this relationship, as Errol eventually comes to accept that where Dolly belongs, and her family, is important - "to you, and me," he says. By the end, Dolly and Errol symbolise a reconciliation between black and white Australia based on mutual respect, not on Dolly having to become someone else to fit into a white Australian world (3).
Annotations:

(1) This paragraph deliberately introduces a new dimension of "home" (choice and generational change) rather than repeating the previous paragraph’s argument — a common weakness in weaker essays is three body paragraphs that all make the same point with different quotes.

(2) Noticing that Dolly’s line echoes Nan Dear’s earlier "it’s ours" is intertextual analysis within the one play — tracing a phrase across generations of characters demonstrates a command of the whole text rather than isolated scenes.

(3) Using the Dolly-Errol relationship as a symbol for broader reconciliation, rather than just a subplot romance, elevates the paragraph from character analysis to thematic analysis, which is what the higher bands of the criteria are looking for.
Conclusion

Looking at all three generations together, Harrison makes the point that home can’t just be a house on Crown land (1). White Australia’s focus on modern features and material comfort ends up looking empty because it comes without family closeness, while the Indigenous characters, even with their differences, keep coming back to land rights, community and love as what really makes a home. Nan Dear holds onto that connection no matter what the government tries to take from her, Gladys fights to have it recognised on her own terms, and Dolly shows that it can survive change and distance without being lost (2). Home, for Harrison, isn’t something white Australia can hand over through better housing policy. It must come with genuine respect for Indigenous identity, land and culture. That’s ultimately what Dolly and Errol’s relationship offers, and what the play argues Australia still should strive to achieve (3).
Annotations:

(1) A strong conclusion returns to the contention’s own language ("home can’t just be a house") rather than introducing it for the first time here — this shows the essay has been building towards this point all along, not arriving at it by accident.

(2) Summarising each generation with one precise verb (holds, fights, shows) is an efficient way to synthesise three paragraphs of evidence into a single sentence without simply repeating quotes.

(3) Ending on what "Australia still should strive to achieve" extends the essay’s implications beyond the play itself, which is a hallmark of a top-band response: the argument matters because it says something about the real world, not just about the text.

Analytical Text Response Essays

Introduction

From the very beginning of your schooling career, you have been introduced to several different methods of writing essays. Although all structures have their advantages and disadvantages, it is essential that you have a fool-proof method of writing essays for your end of year examination.
Having a solid method of writing analytical essays and practicing these throughout the year will result in a strong foundation, enabling you to tackle any essay question with confidence. The key to success lies not only in understanding the content but also in being able to present your ideas in a clear, structured, and coherent manner. With a well-practised approach, you will be able to break down complex texts, analyse them effectively, and construct a well-supported argument that demonstrates both depth and clarity of thought.
In the following, we will explore a structured approach to writing an analytical text response. By familiarising yourself with this framework, you will be better equipped to organise your ideas, manage your time efficiently, and ultimately maximise your performance in the exam.

What is Needed in an Essay

An essay is a structured piece of writing that presents an argument, analysis, or narrative on a particular topic. The purpose of an essay is to communicate a clear, well-reasoned point of view, often backed by evidence and examples. Essays are typically composed of several key parts: an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion. Each section plays a specific role in conveying the writer’s ideas and helping the reader understand and engage with the topic at hand.

Introduction

The introduction serves as the starting point of the essay, setting the stage for the discussion to follow. It introduces the topic, provides necessary background information, and presents the thesis statement, which outlines the main argument or purpose of the essay. A strong introduction grabs the reader’s attention, often through a compelling hook, and establishes the importance of the topic. It should also provide a clear roadmap of what the essay will cover, guiding the reader’s expectations and providing a sense of structure.

Body Paragraphs

The body of an essay is where the main arguments or points are developed and supported with evidence, examples, or analysis. Typically, an essay contains three body paragraphs, although this can vary depending on the length and depth of the topic. Each paragraph should focus on a single aspect of the thesis statement and contribute to the overall argument.

Conclusion

The conclusion is the final part of the essay, where the writer sums up the key points made in the body paragraphs and reinforces the thesis statement. It provides closure to the discussion and leaves a lasting impression on the reader. A strong conclusion restates the main argument in a fresh way, summarising the key insights presented in the essay. It may also offer suggestions for further exploration or suggest the broader implications of the topic.

Planning

Deconstructing the Prompt

When first planning your essay, you must directly respond to the prompt that is presented to you, and consider the key authorial messages that could help you respond with insight. This involves three important steps:

  • Find the key concepts. This will most likely correlate with the key concepts you have studied throughout the unit, you should also make sure to identify synonyms of these concepts too!

  • Find any instructional verbs. These are words that instruct you to answer the prompt in a certain manner, in English the main verbs to look out for is discuss, do you agree, to what extent or how.

  • Identify any expectations/limitations. These ore words that limit what you con include in your essays and set on expectation on what you should be discussing.

Example prompt: ‘The dream sequences in Rainbow’s End are the characters’ only escape from the depressing reality of life.’ Do you agree?
This is a very straightforward prompt—you need to demonstrate a multiple-sided response to what Harrison perceives as the role of dreams and aspirations in her characters’ lives. There are no limits to what should be discussed here, but you are expected to focus your discussion on the ideas of dreams, escape, and reality as they relate to the values, identities, and choices of Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly. Consider the historical context of 1950s assimilationist Australia, racism, class, and the characters’ evolving sense of agency throughout the play.

Contention

Now that you understand what the prompt is asking, we can begin to think about what our interpretation of Harrison’s message is. Let’s consider the first prompt again:
‘The dream sequences in Rainbow’s End are the characters’ only escape from the depressing reality of life.’ Do you agree?
We can approach this in multiple ways. Here are three simplistic interpretations (agree, disagree, somewhere in between):

  • Harrison suggests that dreams offer the characters comfort, but they cannot change the reality of their circumstances.

  • Harrison implies that dreams are the characters’ only refuge from a society that denies them dignity and opportunity.

  • Harrison demonstrates that it is action, not dreaming, that ultimately allows the characters to escape their circumstances.

However, these interpretations are quite general and lack specificity. They don’t tell the reader exactly what you are arguing. Let’s improve them with more precise language and deeper knowledge of the text:

  • Harrison portrays dreams as both sustaining and limiting, suggesting that Gladys’s fantasies of meeting the Queen preserve her dignity while simultaneously revealing how deeply colonial ideals of acceptance have been internalised.

  • Harrison implies that, for a family denied housing, education, and respect, the imagination becomes a vital site of survival and self-worth in a society that offers no material escape.

  • Harrison critiques passive dreaming by charting Gladys’s transformation from daydreamer to activist, suggesting that genuine escape from injustice comes not from fantasy but from finding one’s voice and demanding change.

These improved interpretations use specific language, engage with the complexity of the characters’ motivations, and demonstrate a sophisticated understanding of the play’s ideas.

Extending Sentences - Connectives

To create a ’sophisticated’ idea you need to avoid single ideas, all of the contentions above are singular and lack connections to other ideas. We can connect them together using three simple connectives (and their advanced synonyms!)

Useful Conjunctions and Their Purposes
Conjunction Purpose Synonyms
Conjunction Purpose Synonyms
Because This conjunction explains why the information you have stated is true, demonstrating greater understanding of the author’s purpose. As, as a result of, considering, being, for, through, seeing as.
But This conjunction adds contradictory information, allowing for greater debate. However, nevertheless, yet, despite, even so, nonetheless, except.
So This conjunction allows for inferencing the cause and effect of the statement, which demonstrates greater thinking and ideas about the text. Therefore, consequently, accordingly, leading to.

From here, we can take these singular idea contentions and extend them with these connectives in order to make multi-idea contentions that can create sophisticated links between ideas:

Topic Sentences

Now we can create topic sentences that allow to explore complex ideas linked using BECAUSE, BUT & so.

Authorial Message/Intent - NOT PLOT OR CHAPTER!

The sentence should be focused on an authorial message, not just a concept or character­ make the author active -the most common start is the author+ verb:
E.g Harrison explores/communicates/illustrates etc.

Authorial Verbs
Analytical Verbs for Text Analysis
Conveys Explores Demonstrates Emphasises
Communicates Depicts Illustrates Challenges
Identifies Frames Reveals Highlights
States Implies Suggests Underlines
Prepositional Phrases

Another way to structure this sentence is to use a prepositional phrase at the beginning of the sentence that introduces ideas to vary your sentence structure:

Throughout the text, Harrison explores..

Textual Analysis Phrases and Sentence Starters

Through From the outset In the absence of Under the
By Beyond the Within the To the extent
During Concerning the Without
Specificity and Relevancy

Examiners punish vague or general statements, especially those that lack clear links to the topic or the author’s message. For example:
By emphasising the importance of dreams in the play, Harrison shows that they matter to Gladys.
This is not technically incorrect—but it is far too general and superficial. It doesn’t demonstrate insightful analysis, nor does it show a clear understanding of Harrison’s authorial intent. This kind of vague sentence would not receive full marks.
Here’s an improved version:
By emphasising Gladys’s fantasies of royal recognition, Harrison portrays how dreams function not as an escape from reality, but as a fragile shield of dignity for those denied respect and opportunity by an assimilationist society.
This is better. It is clear, specific, well-structured, and shows awareness of both the theme (dreams vs reality), character motivation, and authorial message. It also directly links to the contention: that dreams sustain the characters, even in circumstances that offer no material escape.
The issue you will face is that there is only so much you can discuss in your paragraph from here, as you have only examined one idea—dreams sustain dignity. To fix this, lets’ go back to an old friend:

Because, But, So - Make a Complex Sentence!

Too often, students fail to complete long paragraphs because they are stuck repeating themselves or have run out of things to say about their topic they introduced. Therefore, if we have two ideas in our topic sentence, we can ’hinge’ these sentences together which demonstrate complex understanding, writing and structure - all of them are key parts of the rubric you are judged on! Here’s an example:

By emphasising Gladys’s daydreams of royal recognition, Harrison conveys how, for a family denied dignity and opportunity, the imagination becomes a vital refuge from an unjust reality—ultimately suggesting that dreams alone cannot deliver change, and that true escape demands the courage to speak and act.

Now, you have two linked ideas:

  • Dreams preserve dignity and hope for the characters in an unjust society.

  • Dreaming alone, without action, cannot deliver genuine change.

This provides a clear structure for an entire paragraph and sets you up to use a hinge sentence later to develop further layers—e.g., how this ’dreaming” can also reveal internalised colonial ideals or delay confrontation with injustice.
By planning your paragraph in this way, you’ll ensure:

  • Specific and sophisticated analysis

  • Strong authorial intent

  • Complex understanding of the characters’ motivations and Harrison’s critique of assimilationist policies in 1950s Australia

Analytical Text Response Structure

Topics

There are three types of questions you may receive on a SAC or exam. These are the ’Discuss’, ’To what extent’ and ’Do you agree?’ statements. These are all tackled in a slightly varying manner.

  • Discuss : This is probably the most common type of prompt that you will encounter. When dealing with such prompts, it is important that you discuss as many aspects of it as possible. One way to improve your discussion here is to include a paragraph that rebuts the prompt, like what you do in your persuasive essays. However, since it is a discuss prompt, you do not need to make the two sides of your argument extremely distinct.

  • To what extent: These topics require you to make a decision. Never say ’it is to some extent’ in your contention. You really need to assign a portion of something. The best way to do this is to start with subordinating conjunctions like ’whilst” or ’although”. To What Extent questions rarely want you to agree or disagree - they want you to explore a shade of grey.

  • How: The ’How’ topics usually focus on the construction of a text - your evidence should contain metalanguage like ’characterisation’ for instance.

Introduction

The introduction is the first piece of your writing that will be examined. For this reason, it should be clear, direct and show your understanding of the topic. The introduction is there to show your examiner how you wish to respond to the set topic.
If your topic contains a quote, ensure that you mention it somewhere in your introduction to show you are addressing the topic.

Structure

An introduction must include the following:

A General Line of introduction

Too often our introductions lack any insight and mainly begin with author+text+background information which has nothing to do with the actual topic. We should attempt to be relevant with our opening statement and discuss why this concept is important.

Introduce the Text, Author and Contention

Now we introduce the text and author and the audience. Then state how it relates to the topic specifically. These need to be connected fluently, when writing we should be relying on connectives to make sure there is a logical flow in our expression.
Depending on the prompt itself, it might be a wise idea to link either/both of characters and setting to the topic of the essay prompt. We can do this as such:

N.B. that the name of the book is always underlined. I.e. Sunset Boulevard or Rainbow’s End.
Contention
A contention is the author’s point of view on the topic. Since you are the author in this scenario, you need to write what your position on the topic. A contention should be short, to the point, and engage with the topic. On this falls the crux of your essay.
Do not be afraid to agree/disagree with a topic. You are allowed to have a conditional contention.

Body Paragraphs

The body paragraphs form majority of your essay. The body paragraphs are the main reasons that the contention your provided is true. Your body paragraphs need to be able to demonstrate your understanding of the text and the fluency of your language. You should ensure that every sentence (and thus every word) brings something towards your argument.

Structure

Below is the structure for a body paragraph using ’Th.E. S.A.M.L.’ Method. This method of writing body paragraphs was developed in order to maximise possible marks. Only the ’TH’ and ’L’ elements of this structure need to be in the order given.

ZZSAML

Basic Example ’Th.E S.A.M’

’Obsession with one’s self is what ultimately leads to the downfall of characters. This can be seen with Norma exclaiming ‘who else?’ when Joe questions her about her script. The script itself is also a representation of Norma’s obsession as she has written several hundred pages where she stars in every scene. Wilder uses this to demonstrate that Norma is unable to move on from anything besides herself. The slow camera pan whenever the script is shown foreshadows Norma’s impending doom which links is inherently linked to her self-absorption. In this way, obsession with self ultimately leads to the downfall of several characters in Sunset Boulevard.’

Writing About Ideas: Adding Adjectives to Nouns

One of the easiest ways of improving your writing is to add adjectives to nouns, this helps us to create an ’idea phrase’. Here is a list of commonly used nouns in analytic writing, a good way is to use these as springboards to finding even more complex and advanced nouns.

We then can pair these ideas with adjectives of degree - these tell us how big, small, frequent, fast or slow something is, here is a list to help build your writing vocabulary:

Hinge Sentences

In your topic sentence, you can introduce two related ideas. This creates room for a more complex, insightful paragraph. But remember: when you shift focus from the first idea to the second, you must signal this change to the reader clearly. This is where the hinge sentence comes in.
A hinge sentence acts like a bridge—it acknowledges what you’ve already explored, then transitions to the second idea you introduced in your topic sentence. Here’s how that works:

By emphasising Gladys’s daydreams of royal recognition, Harrison conveys how, for a family denied dignity and opportunity, the imagination becomes a vital refuge from an unjust reality—ultimately suggesting that dreams alone cannot deliver change, and that true escape demands the courage to speak and act.

Evidence + Analysis of First Idea

In the dream sequences where Gladys imagines being received by the Queen, Harrison frames fantasy not as idle escapism but as a shield of dignity for a woman excluded from the “decent” society she longs to join. Yet Nan Dear’s blunt dismissal—“And what bloody good would that do? Daydreams!” (p. 154)—exposes the limits of this refuge, highlighting how dreaming leaves the family’s material circumstances untouched.

Hinge Sentence (transitioning to Second idea)

However, while Gladys’s dreams preserve her sense of self-worth, Harrison also reveals that fantasy alone cannot deliver her family from injustice.

Analysis of Second Idea

Gladys’s transformation culminates in her address to the Rodney Shire Council, where she demands “proper schooling … The white people too — they need to be educated about us, and our ways” (p. 197). Harrison complicates the notion of escape by showing that it is voice and action, not fantasy, that begin to change the family’s circumstances, suggesting that dreams are only powerful when they inspire agency.
By using a hinge sentence, you show your reader that you’re not just stacking ideas but developing an argument, deepening your interpretation, and showing the author’s nuanced message.

Concluding Dependant Phrases Paired with Analytic Verbs
Concluding Dependant Phrase Analytic Verb
Concluding Dependant Phrase Analytic Verb
Even if Sophocles recognises... questions...
Whilst Sophocles endorses... illustrates...
Whether or not Sophocles implies... reveals...
In order to explore ... Sophocles ... demonstrates...

Conclusion

If you think of a conclusion as less of summarising everything, and more of combining all of these conflicting ideas and messages into one overall conclusive point. We can do this by using the following structure.

Structure

The structure for a conclusion can follow the same structure as an introduction. However, instead of introducing the text, you are concluding your discussion.
Here is an alternative method:
Topic:

‘The dream sequences in Rainbow’s End are the characters’ only escape from the depressing reality of life.’ Do you agree?

Conclusion Structure for Analytical Essay Writing
Purpose in Conclusion Example Sentence
Purpose in Conclusion Example Sentence
Overview of how the whole text demonstrates the key concepts in the essay topic Fundamentally, Rainbow’s End explores the tension between dreams and reality, particularly how fantasy can offer dignity, but not deliverance, to those denied opportunity by an unjust society.
Summary of the complications of this topic Throughout the play, Harrison complicates the notion of escape by showing that while Gladys’s daydreams preserve her sense of self-worth, they leave her family’s circumstances unchanged, challenging the romanticisation of hope without action.
Focus on the key authorial message in conclusive language Therefore, Harrison critiques a society that forces its most marginalised to retreat into fantasy, asserting that genuine change lies not in dreaming of acceptance but in finding the voice to demand it, as Gladys does before the Shire Council.

Here is a list of concluding and verb phrases you can use at the beginning of your conclusion.

Proofreading

Take a Break Before Proofreading

After finishing your essay, your mind is still fresh with ideas, but it may be too familiar with the text to catch all mistakes. Step away from the essay for at least 10–15 minutes. This will help you approach it with fresh eyes and a clearer mindset when you return to review it.

Read Aloud

Reading aloud forces you to slow down and focus on each word. It also helps you hear awkward phrasing or sentences that don’t flow well. Read your essay aloud, paying attention to how each sentence sounds. If something feels off or too long-winded, make adjustments.

Check for Structure and Coherence

A strong essay is logically structured with clear arguments and smooth transitions. Ensure each paragraph has a clear topic sentence. Verify your argument flows logically from one point to the next. Ensure your introduction sets up your essay well and your conclusion summarises without introducing new ideas.

Look for Clarity and Precision

Vague or unclear language can confuse your reader, even if the argument itself is solid. Check if any sentences can be more specific or concise. Remove unnecessary filler words like ’really,’ ’just,’ or ’quite.’ Replace vague words like ’things’ or ’stuff’ with more precise terms.

Verify Your Thesis and Topic Sentences

Your thesis statement should be the central focus of your essay, and topic sentences should align with it. Double-check that:

  • Your thesis is clear and present in the introduction.

  • Each body paragraph clearly supports your thesis with an argument or evidence.

  • Topic sentences match the content of their respective paragraphs.

Proofread for Grammar and Syntax

Grammar mistakes, awkward phrasing, or incorrect sentence structure can weaken the professionalism of your essay. Read through your essay for common mistakes (e.g., subject-verb agreement, incorrect punctuation, run-on sentences). Use tools like Grammarly or even Microsoft Word’s spelling/grammar checker for a quick second look.

Check for Word Choice and Tone

The right word choice ensures your ideas are clear and impactful. Avoid overly complex words unless they are necessary for the context. Ensure you maintain an academic tone throughout the essay. Replace informal language (e.g., ’kids’ or ’stuff’) with more formal, specific terms.

Look for Repetition

Repeating the same word or idea can weaken your essay’s overall impact. Identify words or phrases that appear too often. Use synonyms or restructure sentences to avoid redundancy. Consider varying sentence structures to keep the reader engaged.

Check Punctuation and Spacing

Simple punctuation errors or inconsistent spacing can distract the reader. Verify commas, full stops, and apostrophes are used correctly. Ensure there is proper spacing after full stops and between paragraphs.

Ask Someone Else to Read It

Fresh eyes can often spot mistakes you may have missed. Ask a peer, teacher, or family member to read your essay. Be open to their feedback and use it to improve your writing.

Ensure It Meets the Task Requirements

Your essay should always answer the question and meet the criteria specified in the prompt. Revisit the question to ensure your essay directly addresses it. Check that all key parts of the task are covered and that your argument is relevant.

Creating Texts

Audience

The audience refers to anyone who is viewing, reading or listening to a text.
1. Begin to identify your audience by answering the following questions:

2. What are you writing about?

3. Who would be interested in this topic?

4. Is there more than one audience who might be interested in this?

5. What other texts exist that are like my idea? Who are their audience?

Purpose

Writing to Express

Use your imagination and creativity to explore and convey ideas

Examples include novels, short stories, plays, poems and films.

Usually involves imagined characters, places, situations and events.

Usually contain tension and conflict as characters seek to achieve their coals.

Writing to Explain

Use your imagination and creativity to explore and convey ideas

Examples include novels, short stories, plays, poems and films.

Usually involves imagined characters, places, situations and events.

Usually contain tension and conflict as characters seek to achieve their coals.

Use your imagination and creativity to explore and convey ideas

Examples include novels, short stories, plays, poems and films.

Usually involves imagined characters, places, situations and events.

Usually contain tension and conflict as characters seek to achieve their coals.

Use your imagination and creativity to explore and convey ideas

Personal Journeys

From the Study Design
Explorations of ’life’ or biographical explorations - telling our stories, telling others’ stories, the problem of telling stories, appropriation of stories, who tells the stories and our history, missing stories, marginalised and elevated stories. Students could explore personal milestones, the effects of key events on their lives, or explore these ideas through the eyes of others.
Students who have migrated can explore their stories of movement and disruption. They can explore the expectations of change and the language of a new place and culture.

Writing

  • Writing about personal journeys explores both the physical world and the internal world of oneself and others.

  • Reading and crafting texts about journeys provide opportunities to consider new places, perspectives, and self-discovery.

  • Personal journeys involve various experiences, including challenges, growth, and self-discovery.

  • Writing about personal journeys allows individuals to share unique experiences with others.

REMINDER: You have access to the exam generator and the prompt generator to practice your Section B skills.

Ideas

A personal journey can involve:

  • Travel to a physical place.

  • Psychological or emotional growth and change.

  • A combination of both, where physical travel leads to new insights.

  • Interior journeys may take a long time to occur due to significant events.

  • The most important aspect of a personal journey is change – the individual or group is fundamentally altered by it.
