All of this served not to It’s a sense of empathy, but guilt; A guilt only exacerbated by the prior emotional framing of drug users as ‘vulnerable.’ This component essence of utilising first empathy and then guilts creates a structure that is particularly Difficult to read but. The author particularly the conservative one to which this section is addressed, is caught in a ‘catch twenty-two’ wherein this cannot take a position of moral integrity, without conceding the value of a safe injecting facility.
The author then transitions to a sensitive voice, criticising the governments’s ‘action plan’ for its prioritisation of treatment over relief. The author is again controversial, and involves a quote from his ‘mate’, asserting the’ betrayal’. This strengthens his case as not just the testimony of one, the betrayal of an entire community of stakeholders. The reader is then positioned to perceive the authors view as one of relevance and authority. This further results by the political lens next taking, wherein the author elects to admire to the reader that he has voted for his government and party for years. This again is most effective on a reader whom does not share his view, but may share his vote. By asserting a common ground, readers positional to assess their own biases, and furthers the personal connection constructed at the opinion of the peaceful stop the author then flips the politicisation on its head, criticising where votes are sound From a moral view. This again leverages a sense of moral guilt, that the government would prioritise ‘shop owners’ over doing ‘things for the community’. These statements escalate on the guilt restored previously frankly the issue as a single moral dichotomy in the readers eye.
The author asserts the efficacy of safe injecting rooms and criticises of rejection as a betrayal of vulnerable people. Believing tone shift, from gathering empathy to explicit criticism the author seeks to frame the issue in a moral and emotional light; Hoping to persuade the less vehement opposition amongst Melburnians.

The Exam

Exam Format

The VCE English exam is a three-hour exam, plus 15 minutes of reading time. It has three sections, and you are required to complete all three sections. Each section is worth the same number of marks, so you should spend the same amount of time on each.

Exam Specifications

Overall Conditions

  • The examination will be sat at a time and date to be set annually by the Victorian Curriculum and Assessment Authority (VCAA). VCAA examination rules will apply.

  • There will be 15 minutes of reading time and 3 hours of writing time.

  • The examination will be assessed by a panel appointed by the VCAA.

  • The examination will contribute 50 per cent to the study score.

Format

The Task Book will be sixteen pages in length. The front page contains the general cover notes and instructions, including rules about what you can and can’t take into the exam.
An English and/or bilingual printed dictionary is permitted.
Mobile Phones and/pr any unauthrosied electronic devices are not permitted.

Specifications from VCAA:

  • The examination will be in the form of a Task Book.

  • The examination will consist of three sections.

  • Section A will consist of an analytical response to a text and will be worth a total of 20 marks.

  • Students will be required to write an analytical response to one of the two texts studied from List 1 of the VCE English and English as an Additional Language (EAL) Text List for the year in question. There will be two topics for each of the 20 texts on List 1. Students will be required to write a response to one topic on one text.

  • Student responses should demonstrate knowledge and understanding of the text, its structure and the ideas, concerns and values raised by the topic, and support their response with close reference to the text. If the selected text is a collection of poetry or short stories, students may write on several poems or stories, or on at least two in close detail.

  • The response will be assessed according to the assessment criteria specified on the following page.

  • Section B will consist of creating a text and will be worth a total of 20 marks.

  • Students will be required to create one written text. For the purposes of this task, a ‘written text’ does not include song, poetry or verse. In creating their text, students must use the title provided, relevant idea(s) from at least one unseen stimulus and be connected to one of the Frameworks of Ideas. The text must be developed with a clear purpose(s) – to explain, to express, to argue, to reflect – and should be an effective and cohesive piece of writing. Students should demonstrate fluency through the selection of vocabulary, text structure(s) and language features, and employ an appropriate voice in their text.

  • The place of mentor texts is to provide access to ideas and inform the teaching of writing, but students will not be required to make explicit reference to them in the examination.

  • The text will be assessed according to the assessment criteria specified below. Section B will be worth a total of 20 marks.

  • Section C will consist of an analysis of argument and language and will be worth a total of 20 marks.

  • Students will be required to analyse the use of argument(s) and language to persuade an intended audience to share the point of view expressed in an unseen persuasive text. For the purposes of this task, ‘language’ refers to written and spoken language, and ‘visuals’ refers to images and graphics. Students will not be required to evaluate the relative merits or successes of argument(s).

  • The response will be assessed according to the assessment criteria specified below.

  • Section C will be worth a total of 20 marks.

  • The total marks for the examination will be 60.

  • Answers to Section A, B and C are to be recorded in the Answer Book.

Strategies for Addressing the Requirements

Section A

In Section A you will write an essay that responds to one of the two given topics on a text you have studied. You can choose to write on either your Unit 1 text or your Unit 4 text. In your answer book you will need to identify the text on which you are writing and the topic (i or ii) you are responding to by shading the relevant box.
Spend a few minutes unpacking the topic you feel most comfortable responding to. Look carefully at each term in the topic and make sure you understand its meaning and significance. Use the strategies for identifying topic words, direction words and limiting words outlined on pages 48–50 in English Year 12.
Spend about 5 minutes writing a brief plan that includes your contention, three or four topic sentences and one or two pieces of textual evidence for each topic sentence.
The following strategies will help you to address the Section A requirements and criteria.

  • Address the topic in full. Strong responses take account of every word in the topic, including the limiting words, rather than writing about one key term in a general way.

  • Use your dictionary. Make sure you understand the meanings of all the words in the topic. Your dictionary will also give you some synonyms for key terms in the topic, and using these will give your writing more variety and precision.

  • Consider a range of textual features. Don’t limit your reasons and evidence to just what the characters say and do. While authors do use characters to explore ideas, they also use features such as narrative voice, structure, setting and imagery. Film techniques should be referred to when analysing a film, and an analysis of poetry will be enhanced by a consideration of poetic form and techniques. Discussing these features shows a deeper understanding of the text and a more sophisticated response to the topic.

  • Integrate quotes and explain their significance. It is useful to include some direct quotes, as this is part of how you show your ‘knowledge and understanding of the text’ (criterion 2 for Section A). However, make sure you incorporate quotes into your discussion and thoroughly explain how they support the point you are making. If you use a quote and then mention it on its own, or place it at the end of a sentence without further explanation, it will be unclear to your assessor how the quote helps to advance your argument.

  • Write precisely and expressively. Develop a wide vocabulary during the year by creating word banks (see the examples on pages 58–63 of English Year 12) and use a variety of words in your practice essays, so by the time you sit the exam you can make effective vocabulary choices in all your responses.

Section B

In Section B you will write a text that responds to a given title and at least one piece of stimulus material. In the answer book, you will need to shade the box of your chosen Framework, and the box (or boxes) corresponding to the stimulus (or stimuli) you have responded to.
The following strategies will help you to address the Section B criteria and weave the stimuli and title into your creative piece.

  • Ensure your writing is clearly relevant to your chosen Framework. This involves integrating concepts and perspectives that you’ve explored during the year. When you are revising in preparation for the exam, refer to your notes and written pieces to stay aligned with the core ideas of the Framework.

  • Integrate the title and at least one stimulus into your writing. You must use at least one of the stimuli. The stimulus should not just be referenced superficially; it should be woven into the fabric of your piece. This could mean, for example, using a text stimulus as the basis for a central idea; using a text stimulus as a direct quote at a key point in your text; or drawing inspiration from a visual stimulus to set a scene. Similarly, the title provided for your Framework should provide an idea, a character arc, a central image or another key element of your writing.

  • Give your piece cohesion and structure. Your piece should have a clear beginning, middle and end, regardless of whether it’s a narrative, an argument or a reflective piece. Each part should seamlessly transition into the next, maintaining a logical flow of ideas.

  • Make appropriate use of language, style and voice. The language and style of your writing should suit the purpose and audience. This includes choosing the most appropriate vocabulary, tone and sentence structures. In your Unit 3 drafts and assessment pieces, you will have worked hard to develop your own unique voice. You likely have a preferred form of writing (fiction or nonfiction, story, article etc.) and you should lean into that during the exam.

  • Write with fluency and expression. Your writing should read smoothly, with a natural flow of ideas and language. This can be achieved by varying your sentence structures, giving your writing a subtle rhythm and clearly articulating your ideas. Transitions between paragraphs or sections should be logical and easy for the reader to follow.

Section C

In Section C you will analyse a text in which the writer seeks to persuade a particular audience to agree with their point of view on an issue. The text will be primarily written but will also contain one or more visual elements.
This task has been part of the VCE English exam for many years and as part of your preparation you may use past exam papers as sources of practice material. In some cases you will find that the task material consists of more than one written piece, but the exam specifications for 2024–2027 indicate that there will only be a single ‘unseen persuasive text’ in the English exams during this period.
The following strategies will help you to address the Section C requirements and criteria.

  • Understand the contention. What, essentially, is the writer saying? An accurate understanding of the writer’s central argument underpins your analysis. Clearly state the writer’s contention in your introduction.

  • Read the background information carefully. This is where you will find out the intended audience and the context for the text. Your analysis will be strengthened by a consideration of how the writer’s choices of argument and language are shaped by their particular audience and context (see the final bullet point below).

  • Examine the sequencing of the argument. Writers will arrange their material and their points of argument in a particular order so as to engage their intended audience and influence their thoughts and feelings. Look at the overall shape of the argument, then drill down into the specific points and language choices. Shifts in tone are important aspects of the structure, too.

  • Examine language choices. Writers make deliberate choices of words to add impact to their arguments and to evoke the audience’s emotions. Identify words with strong positive or negative connotations and associations and spend some time in your analysis ‘unpacking’ their likely effects on the audience. You will not have time to analyse every use of language in the text, but discuss a representative sample of language choices.

  • Use effective verbs. In your analysis of a persuasive text, verbs enable you to describe what the writer is doing and how they are doing it. Repeating the verbs ‘positions’ and ‘persuades’ doesn’t show your understanding of how argument and language are being used. Verbs such as ‘reinforces’, ‘emphasises’, ‘instils’, ‘elicits’ and ‘undermines’ can be used to show your understanding of how a writer is attempting to persuade. See the verbs in the activity on page 210 of English Year 12 for some other examples.

  • Analyse how argument, language and visuals are working together. Don’t treat these as separate elements of the text, since an audience’s response will be a result of how all these elements are working in combination. Look at how language is used to present an argument, and how an image complements and reinforces particular reasons or pieces of evidence.

Exam Assessment Criteria

From VCAA’s documentation.

Section A will be assessed against the following criteria:

  • knowledge and understanding of the text, its structure, and the ideas, concerns and values it explores

  • development of a coherent analysis in response to the topic

  • use of evidence from the text to support the analysis

  • use of fluent expression through appropriate use of vocabulary and conventions of Standard Australian English

Section B will be assessed against the following criteria:

  • use of relevant idea(s) drawn from one Framework of Ideas, the title provided and at least one piece of stimulus material

  • creation of a cohesive text that connects to a clear purpose(s) and incorporates an appropriate voice

  • use of suitable text structure(s) and language features to create a text

  • use of fluent expression, including the appropriate use of vocabulary

Section C will be assessed against the following criteria:

  • understanding of contention, argument(s), and point of view

  • analysis of the ways in which written and spoken language and visuals are used to present an argument(s) and to persuade an intended audience

  • use of evidence from the text to support the analysis

  • use of fluent expression through appropriate use of vocabulary and conventions of Standard Australian English

Assessors mark holistically, relating student performance to the published criteria and ranking students over the full range of marks available. Determination of the mark is assisted by the descriptors in ‘Expected qualities for the mark range’. These descriptors have been written to reflect the level of achievement expected at a particular mark or mark range. Both the criteria and the descriptors are fully explored and directly related to the range of student responses during the assessor training process.
For Section B, the Framework of Ideas, textual forms, audience and purpose are explained in the section ‘Framework of Ideas’ on pages 22 and 23 of the VCE English and English as an Additional Language Study Design 2024–2027. These concepts underpin the treatment of key knowledge and key skills, and they are an explicit criterion in the expected qualities for Section B.

How the Marker Reads Your Essay

Examiners do not read your essay the way a friendly teacher does – they read it in passes, testing it against the criteria. Knowing the passes lets you write for them.

The Six Passes

First, the scope audit: the topic is broken into its explicit and implicit parts, including modifying words (“unrelenting cruelty”, not just “cruelty”) and both halves of a double-barrelled prompt. A dimension you never address keeps you out of the 8–10 range. Second, the summary filter: every sentence is silently sorted into retelling, explaining, or analysing how the author constructs meaning – and if retelling dominates, the essay cannot reach the upper band no matter how fluent it is. Third, the topic-sentence thread: conceptual topic sentences that build a progressive argument separate 9–10 essays from 7–8 essays that give three parallel examples of one idea. Fourth, the authorial hand: markers look for sentences whose subject is the author (“Harrison constructs…”, “Wilder juxtaposes…”) – writing about characters as though they are real people is a mid-band signature. Fifth, evidence gravity: quotes that stand alone as their own sentences read mid-band; short quotes woven into your own grammar, drawn from across the whole text, read upper-band. Sixth, the gap analysis: the difference between your mark and the next band is always something specific and nameable – which is why good feedback tells you what was missing, not just what was there.

What the Bands Feel Like

A 5 is relevant but basic: simple structure, simple vocabulary, evidence that supports plot points. A 7 is clear and detailed, uses evidence effectively, but stays at the level of what the text shows rather than how and why it was built that way. A 9 or 10 is sophisticated and perceptive: it reframes the topic conceptually, treats the text as a construction, uses precise metalanguage correctly, and integrates evidence so smoothly the quotation marks could be removed without breaking the sentence.

Section-Specific Traps

In Section A, a contention that simply agrees or disagrees without a bigger conceptual claim caps the essay in the middle band, and ignoring the text’s social and historical values keeps the top band out of reach. In Section B, a competent story that never engages the Framework’s ideas sits mid-band, as do clichéd premises and unearned endings – while adapted echoes of your mentor texts signal studied craft. In Section C, “the readers” is a mid-band audience; the upper band names who the writer targets and what they are being positioned to think, feel and do. Naming a technique without explaining its function for that specific audience is label-picking, and it caps the analysis no matter how many techniques you spot. Track the argument’s arc and tonal shifts; treat any visual as part of the argument, not a separate exhibit.

Exam Checklists

General Checklist for Section A Response

Textual Understanding and Interpretation

  • Have I demonstrated a clear and accurate understanding of the text?

  • Have I explored the ideas, concerns, and values present in the text?

  • Have I considered the structure and language used in the text to convey meaning?

  • Have I shown awareness of the complexities or subtleties in the text’s ideas?

Response to the Topic

  • Have I clearly addressed the essay topic throughout my response?

  • Have I interpreted the implications of the topic (not just the literal meaning)?

  • Have I developed a relevant and thoughtful argument or discussion?

  • Have I used an appropriate strategy to explore the topic (e.g., thematic, character-based, structural)?

Use of Evidence

  • Have I supported my ideas with specific and relevant evidence from the text?

  • Have I explained how my evidence supports my argument?

  • Have I used quotations effectively and accurately?

Organisation and Coherence

  • Is my essay well-structured, with a clear introduction, body, and conclusion?

  • Are my ideas logically sequenced and connected?

  • Have I written clear topic sentences and developed each paragraph?

Language Use

  • Is my language accurate, appropriate, and mostly free of errors?

  • Have I used precise and expressive vocabulary to convey meaning?

  • Is my writing fluent and confident in tone?

General Checklist for Section B Response

Engagement with Ideas and Stimulus

  • Have I considered the ideas raised by the title and stimulus in a thoughtful and meaningful way?

  • Have I connected these ideas to a clear and relevant Framework of Ideas?

  • Have I explored the implications or complexities of the ideas, rather than treating them superficially?

Purpose and Voice

  • Have I clearly established the purpose(s) of my text?

  • Have I maintained an appropriate and consistent voice throughout the piece?

  • Is the form of the piece suited to the intended audience and purpose (e.g., speech, monologue, letter)?

Control of Language

  • Have I used language that is clear, expressive, and appropriate for the form?

  • Have I used a rich and precise vocabulary to strengthen my writing?

  • Have I varied sentence structure and language features to create impact and cohesion?

Control of Text Structure

  • Is my writing well-organised and easy to follow?

  • Have I structured the text purposefully, with a clear beginning, middle, and end?

  • Have I used paragraphs and transitions effectively to guide the reader?

General Checklist for Section C Response

Understanding of Argument and Contention

  • Have I accurately identified the contention of the text?

  • Have I understood how the argument(s) are structured and developed across the text?

  • Have I recognised the overall point of view being expressed by the author/speaker?

Analysis of Persuasive Language and Visuals

  • Have I analysed how language features (written or spoken) are used to persuade the intended audience?

  • Have I explained how visuals (if any) support or enhance the written or spoken argument?

  • Have I discussed how these elements work together to construct the overall persuasive strategy?

Expression and Language Use

  • Have I used language that is clear, precise, and appropriate to an analytical task?

  • Have I written fluently and used analytical vocabulary confidently?

  • Have I maintained a consistent and formal tone suitable for academic analysis?

Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment Report

The 2024 VCE English exam, the first under the revised study design, demonstrated impressive student engagement and strong outcomes. According to the VCAA report, students were generally well prepared and confident. However, the report also identified key areas for improvement. This guide breaks down practical strategies for success in each section of the exam, grounded in the report’s findings.

General Exam Advice

What Worked

  • Students clearly understood the structure and expectations of the exam.

  • Many responses demonstrated well-developed writing and analytical skills.

What Needs Improvement

  • Some students forgot to clearly label their chosen text (Section A) or Framework of Ideas (Section B).

  • Some responses misinterpreted the prompt or overlooked key words in the question.

Practical Tips

  • Label your work clearly. Always begin with:
    Section A: ’Text: Macbeth | Prompt: ‘Ambition leads to downfall. Do you agree?’”
    Section B: ’Title: Resist and Persist | Framework: Protest”

  • Build your vocabulary. Familiarise yourself with words like inescapable, perceptions, central concern, implication, etc.

  • Select the best-fit prompt. Choose the one that allows you to demonstrate your strongest understanding, not just the one that sounds easiest.

Section A: Analytical Response to a Text

Your task: Write a structured essay in response to one of two prompts for your studied text.

What Worked

  • High-scoring responses interpreted the topic with nuance and clarity.

  • Students demonstrated a strong understanding of authorial intent.

  • Responses were well structured and supported with relevant textual evidence.

What Needs Improvement

  • Some students only responded to part of the question (e.g. discussing ’youth” but not ’infatuation with youth”).

  • Others summarised the plot or characters instead of analysing the text’s ideas and values.

Practical Tips

Section A - Effective vs Ineffective Approaches
Effective Approach Ineffective Approach
Interpreting all keywords in the topic Ignoring modifiers (e.g. “unrelenting cruelty” vs “cruelty”)
Using a clear “what/how/why” structure Listing unrelated examples
Connecting ideas across paragraphs Treating each paragraph as a standalone point
Focusing on how the author constructs meaning Just retelling the storyline

’ Tip: Write practice introductions where you break down the prompt. Ask:

  • What is the main idea or concept?

  • Are there two ideas in relationship?

  • What are the implications or assumptions?

Section B - Creating a Text (Framework of Ideas)

Your task: Create an original piece of writing that responds to a title and stimulus, grounded in one of the four Frameworks of Ideas.

What Worked

  • High-scoring pieces treated the title and stimulus as central, not secondary.

  • Strong voice, clear purpose, and tailored language made the writing stand out.

  • Responses showed thoughtful exploration of ideas relevant to the Framework.

What Needs Improvement

  • Some students ignored or misunderstood the Framework (e.g. writing about sports under the Protest framework).

  • Others forced pre-prepared stories to fit the title.

  • Lack of planning resulted in unstructured or unfocused pieces.

Practical Strategies

Planning Method

  • Framework: Personal Journeys

  • Title: Finding My Way

  • Stimulus: Butterfly emerging from a cocoon

  • Purpose: Reflective

  • Audience: Teenagers navigating cultural identity

  • Text Type: Memoir

Tips for Strong Writing

  • Use recurring imagery or motifs to strengthen cohesion.

  • Match tone to purpose: persuasive, reflective, emotive, or narrative.

  • End with intention: resolution, reflection, or call to action.

Section C - Analysing Argument

Your task: Analyse how a written and visual text positions its audience.

What Worked

  • Identifying the author’s contention and target audience.

  • Analysing tone, persuasive language, structure, and visuals.

  • Exploring how language is used to shape reader response.

What Needs Improvement

  • Avoid retelling the article.

  • Go beyond spotting techniques; explain their effect and purpose.

Practical Strategies

Section B: Effective Strategies and Examples
Strategy Example
Identify contention and purpose ’The writer argues that vaping is a growing public health crisis and seeks to alarm parents.”
Link language to effect ’By using the phrase ‘a ticking time bomb’, the writer creates a sense of urgency and panic.”
Analyse visuals ’The image of a distressed child reinforces the emotional appeal used in the article’s opening.”
Use precise metalanguage Appeal to fear, inclusive language, repetition, expert opinion, etc.

Final Exam Checklist

  • Have I practised responding to a range of prompts and titles?

  • Do I clearly understand my Frameworks of Ideas?

  • Can I write for a clear audience and purpose?

  • Have I reviewed unfamiliar vocabulary and metalanguage?

  • Can I plan and write all three sections under timed conditions?

Final Advice

The 2026 exam will reward students who think deeply, write purposefully, and tailor their writing to the demands of the task. Whether you’re analysing a text, creating your own piece, or evaluating argument and language, success lies in showing insight, clarity, and control.
Strong writing does not come from memorisation—it comes from understanding. Prepare thoughtfully, practise deliberately, and revise reflectively.

Effectively Studying For Exams

Assess your current study habits

Identifying strengths and weaknesses

Take a few minutes to jot down what’s working well in your current study routine. What areas could use some improvement? Consider factors like time management, focus, and understanding of different subjects.

Setting realistic goals

Based on your assessment, you could start by setting 2-3 specific, achievable goals for improving your study habits. Make these goals SMART: Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, and Time-bound. Write them down and place them somewhere visible as a daily reminder.

Create a personalised study schedule

Now that you know where you stand, it’s time to map out a path to exam success. Think of your study schedule as a roadmap to help you navigate your way through VCE studies.

Time management tips

List all your subjects and the topics you need to cover for each. Assess how much time you have until your exams. Consider prioritising subjects based on difficulty and your current performance. Using a study schedule template Remember: Be realistic with your schedule. If you’re not a morning person, a sunrise study session may not suit you. Try to work with your natural rhythms, and not against them.

Find a study schedule template that works for you – digital or physical, whichever works best.

Block out fixed commitments like school, extracurriculars, and family time. Allocate study blocks for each subject, ensuring a mix of subjects each day. Don’t forget to schedule breaks and ’me time’ to avoid feelings of burnout.

Organise your study materials

A cluttered space can contribute to a cluttered mind. Here are some strategies to effectively organise your study materials.

Gathering resources

Collect all your textbooks, notes, and past papers for each subject. Organise digital resources into clearly labelled folders on your computer. Consider creating a ’study toolkit’ with essentials like highlighters, sticky notes, and index cards.

Creating a study-friendly environment

Find a quiet, well-lit space where you can focus without distractions. Ensure you have a comfortable chair and desk at the right height. Keep your study area clean and organised – a tidy space can do wonders for your concentration.

Implement active recall and spaced repetition

Below are some tried and true study techniques you might like to explore.

The power of self-testing

After studying a topic, close your books and try to recall as much as you can. Create flashcards for key concepts and test yourself regularly. Explain concepts out loud as if you’re teaching someone else.

Spacing out your study sessions

Avoid cramming by spreading out your study sessions over time. Review material at increasing intervals: e.g. 1 day later, 3 days later, 1 week later, etc.

Utilise practice exams and past papers

Another way to effectively prepare for exams is by practising past exams. Here are some techniques you might find helpful.

Simulating exam conditions

Find past papers or practice exams for your subjects. Set up a quiet space and time yourself as you would in a real exam. Avoid using your notes or textbooks during these practice sessions.

Analysing your performance

Mark your practice exams using the relevant marking schemes, if available. Identify areas where you lost marks and focus on improving these in your study sessions. Keep a log of common mistakes to avoid repeating them in the real exam.

Develop a healthy study-life balance

It’s important to look after yourself and practice self-care during this time. Find what works best for you, or try our ideas below.

Incorporating breaks and relaxation

Use techniques like the Pomodoro method: 25 minutes of focused study followed by a 5-minute break. Schedule longer breaks for activities you enjoy – maybe a quick game of basketball or a chat with friends. Practice mindfulness or meditation to help manage stress and improve focus.

Maintaining physical and mental well-being

Aim for 7-9 hours of sleep per night – your brain consolidates memories and learning during sleep. Incorporate regular exercise to improve your mood and reduce stress. Eat a balanced diet with brain-boosting foods like blueberries, nuts, and fatty fish.

Collaborate with study groups

Two heads are better than one, and a study group can be a powerhouse of knowledge, inspiration and motivation.

The benefits of peer learning

Explaining concepts to others can help solidify your own understanding. Gain new perspectives and insights from your peers. Stay motivated and accountable with group study sessions.

Organising effective study sessions

Set clear objectives for each study session. Use tools like shared Google Docs or virtual whiteboards for collaborative note-taking.

Seek help when needed

Remember, asking for help isn’t a sign of weakness.

Utilising teacher support

Don’t hesitate to approach your teachers with specific questions. Attend any extra help sessions offered by your school or community. Ask for feedback on your work.

Exploring additional resources

Explore online tutorials or video explanations for challenging topics. Consider getting a tutor for subjects where you’re really struggling. Join online forums or study groups for your specific subjects.

Fine-tune your exam day strategy

When your exams are approaching – make sure you’re feeling prepared.

Pre-exam preparation

Get a good night’s sleep before the exam. Eat a nutritious breakfast to fuel your brain. Arrive at the exam venue early to avoid last-minute stress.

During the exam tactics

Read through the entire paper before starting to answer questions. Manage your time wisely – allocate minutes to each section based on mark values. If you get stuck on a question, move on and come back to it later.
